## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 **Critical security concerns** - This deliberately vulnerable web shop lets anonymous visitors take over administrator accounts and run commands on the server. Acceptable only as an isolated test target; it must never hold real data or be reachable from untrusted networks.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 3 · 🟠 High: 23 · 🟡 Medium: 46 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 72**<br/>**Findings register:** 134 · 🔴 Critical: 4 · 🟠 High: 46 · 🟡 Medium: 84 - the distribution above counts insecure-practice findings under their weakness; see [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register)<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 49 of 71 finding(s) confirmed in code<br/>**Weakness classes:** 48 (41 implementation, 7 design) - see [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)

<br/>

**Security concerns behind this assessment:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Login bypass and database theft** — SQL injection in the login form and product search lets an unauthenticated visitor sign in as an administrator and read the database. *(🔴 [F-009](#f-009), 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Anyone can register as administrator** — Mass assignment on account creation lets an anonymous visitor choose the administrator role for a new account, and set another user's recovery answer. *(🟠 [F-059](#f-059), 🟠 [F-060](#f-060), 🟠 [F-061](#f-061), 🟡 [F-143](#f-143) → [W-006](#w-006))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Other customers' records read and changed** — Missing ownership checks (IDOR) let any signed-in customer read or change another customer's data. *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005) → [W-005](#w-005))* — left open by the evidence review: 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Broken authorization
- **Forged logins for any user** — Login tokens are verified loosely and their signing key sits in the source code, so anyone with the code can impersonate any user. *(🟠 [F-013](#f-013), 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) → [W-003](#w-003), [W-008](#w-008))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain — left open by the evidence review: 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist
- **Server takeover through a profile field** — A signed-in user can store a malicious username that runs as code on the server (remote code execution). *(🔴 [F-010](#f-010) → [W-001](#w-001))*

</blockquote>

<br/>

Until these gaps are closed, the system should stay on a closed network with disposable data and no real user accounts.

**About this assessment:** An AI-assisted threat model derived from the implementation of Juice Shop. It reconstructs the implemented architecture (9 components and 5 external services, see [§2](#2-architecture-diagrams)) and identifies threats and control gaps in it.


**Method and limits:** Automated static analysis of code and configuration, not a pentest or a team threat-modeling session; Low and Informational findings not reported (threshold: medium) - see [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope).

### Remediation Priorities

The root causes behind the findings, ranked by severity, each with the measures to do first. The lasting fix under a root cause removes it and every finding it causes. Every mitigation that fixes a Critical finding is listed. Details in the [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) and the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

### Open Questions for the Team

The code cannot answer these questions; the people who own the business and the deployment can. Each question states under it what its answer changes.

- _How critical are User credentials and JWT session tokens to the business, and what harm would their disclosure, manipulation or unavailability cause?_
  The answer weights the impact rating and fix order of every finding that reaches these assets.
- _Which sensitive business records can the production database account used by Juice Shop Express Server or Authentication & Session Surface access?_ ([W-002](#w-002): [F-003](#f-003), [F-009](#f-009))
  Personal or regulated data in reach raises both the breach impact and any notification duty; a narrowly scoped account limits the blast radius.
- _If an attacker runs code in Juice Shop Express Server, which other services, secrets or credentials can that process reach?_ ([F-010](#f-010), [F-050](#f-050) (unproven))
  Every reachable service or credential extends this from one component to a wider compromise and raises the priority of isolating that process; an isolated process contains it.

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview**

At runtime, Juice Shop has 8 components in 3 layers and exchanges data with 5 external services. Figure 1a shows these data flows and where each of the 6 attack scenarios below begins. The complete boundary catalogue remains in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1.svg" alt="Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview"></picture>

**Build pipeline**, not drawn in this runtime view: GitHub Actions, GitLab CI.  Supply-Chain / Build Attacker → [Figure 1b](#figure-1b).

Role access and finding assignments are listed in [Identified Actors](#identified-actors).

**Actor grouping.** Regular self-registered users are grouped with anonymous internet attackers because registration is open. Each finding retains its login and privilege requirements.

<a id="figure-1b"></a>

**Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build**

Juice Shop builds in GitHub Actions, GitLab CI. Figure 1b shows the inputs, build systems and release artifacts the repository evidences, with 8 findings attached to them. The highlighted path follows 🟡 [F-073](#f-073) — Untrusted npm Install/Postinstall Scripts Enabled as far as the repository evidences it.

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1b-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1b.svg" alt="Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build"></picture>

**Figure 2 - Attack Routes and Impact**

Each numbered route names one example finding from the corresponding Top Threats group with its access prerequisite, the register weakness it is linked to, and the group's potential business harm, which depends on deployment and affected assets. W-IDs in parentheses link the example to an existing weakness. The attack step, affected component and technical consequence are listed in the findings register. Red arrows indicate attacks; dashed red arrows involve a victim; grey arrows lead to potential harm.

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure2-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure2.svg" alt="Figure 2 - Actors, attack routes, weaknesses and impact"></picture>

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above.

- **Internet Attacker** — can self-register a regular account; drives ① Broken Authorization & Access Control, ② Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ③ Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval), ④ Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ⑤ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure, ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.

**6 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances by severity under their component, each linked to the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register), and on screen an ID shows its title when you point at it; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence; *Primary Measures* names the most important measures for this threat, most important first, one per root cause. All measures are in the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

_STRIDE: S spoofing · T tampering · R repudiation · I information disclosure · D denial of service · E elevation of privilege. Risk, findings, components, impact and primary measures are derived deterministically; only the one-line weakness description is authored._

**Verified attack chains.** 11 fully viable ([AC-T-003](#ac-t-003), [AC-T-005](#ac-t-005), [AC-B-001](#ac-b-001), [AC-B-002](#ac-b-002), [AC-B-003](#ac-b-003), [AC-B-007](#ac-b-007), [AC-T-004](#ac-t-004), [AC-T-007](#ac-t-007), [AC-B-005](#ac-b-005), [MODEL-AC-003](#model-ac-003), [MODEL-AC-002](#model-ac-002)); 2 partially blocked ([AC-T-002](#ac-t-002), [AC-T-006](#ac-t-006)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

### AI / LLM Exposure

This system embeds an LLM/AI surface (an LLM/AI integration); the risks below are architectural - they follow from how untrusted input reaches the model's prompt, tools, and outputs. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) Prompt Injection** — Untrusted user input reaches the LLM prompt/context without sufficient trust separation, letting an attacker override system instructions, redirect tool calls, or coerce unintended model behaviour. _([C-04](#c-04) — Juice Shop Express Server, [C-05](#c-05) — Angular Frontend)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — Client-supplied chat history passed to model without role _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-058](#f-058) — LLM coupon tool lacks server-side discount _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-087](#f-087) — Stored product reviews returned as tool results enable _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI06](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-090](#f-090) — Chatbot auto-sends attacker-supplied _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-144](#f-144) — Chatbot coupon tool issues any model-chosen discount

- **[LLM05](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm05/) Improper Output Handling** — LLM-generated output flows into a downstream sink (browser, shell, query, eval) without validation or encoding, turning a model completion into an injection vector. _([C-04](#c-04) — Juice Shop Express Server)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-058](#f-058) — LLM coupon tool lacks server-side discount _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

- **[LLM06](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm06/) Excessive Agency** — The LLM is granted tool/function-calling authority without a secondary authorization boundary, so a manipulated model turn can invoke privileged actions on the user's behalf. _([C-04](#c-04) — Juice Shop Express Server)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Chatbot order lookup trusts identity from an unverified JWT _([ASI03](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-058](#f-058) — LLM coupon tool lacks server-side discount _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-144](#f-144) — Chatbot coupon tool issues any model-chosen discount

- **[LLM08](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm08/) Vector and Embedding Weaknesses** — Retrieval/embedding inputs are not isolated by trust, letting injected documents or queries steer retrieval-augmented responses or leak indexed content. _([C-04](#c-04) — Juice Shop Express Server)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Chatbot order lookup trusts identity from an unverified JWT _([ASI03](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — The LLM endpoint imposes no authentication, rate, or quota boundary, so any client can drive unbounded model invocations — uncontrolled provider cost and denial of service for legitimate users. _([C-04](#c-04) — Juice Shop Express Server)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-049](#f-049) — Unbounded LLM consumption on unauthenticated chat endpoint

- **[LLM07](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm07/) System Prompt Leakage** — The system prompt — carrying internal policy, tool rules, or secrets — is extractable through conversational manipulation, exposing the tool capability surface and internal business logic encoded in it. _([C-04](#c-04) — Juice Shop Express Server)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-101](#f-101) — Confidential business rule embedded in extractable system

### Operational Strengths

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

<colgroup><col width="18%" style="width:18%"><col width="32%" style="width:32%"><col width="14%" style="width:14%"><col width="36%" style="width:36%"></colgroup>
<thead><tr><th>Strength</th><th>What's in Place</th><th>Effectiveness</th><th>Open gaps</th></tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Observability &amp; Audit</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Runtime visibility - access logging, audit trails, and operational telemetry for post-incident review.</em><br/>HTTP access logging<br/>Error handling and logging</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">✅ Adequate</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Hardened HTTP Stack</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Browser-facing HTTP hardening — security headers, cookie flags, cross-origin policy, and abuse-protection limits.</em><br/>Socket.IO CORS origin restriction<br/>Rate limiting</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">3 Medium findings remain open:<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-069">F-069</a> — OAuth implicit flow without state, nonce or PKCE<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-080">F-080</a> — Cookie-authenticated profile update<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-090">F-090</a> — Chatbot auto-sends attacker-supplied</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>


**Bottom line:** These controls narrow specific attack surfaces but none eliminates a Critical finding on its own.

## Critical Findings by Root Cause

4 of 4 Critical findings trace back to 3 root causes; fixing a root cause addresses every finding linked to it.

```mermaid
graph LR
    GOAL["4 Critical findings"]:::goal
    W001["W-001<br/>Application data crosses into executable code"]:::rootcause
    T010["F-010 - Server-side code execution from stored username<br/>userProfile.ts"]:::leaf
    W002["W-002<br/>Database access relies on concatenated queries"]:::rootcause
    T003["F-003 - SQL injection in product search query<br/>search.ts"]:::leaf
    T009["F-009 - SQL injection in login credential query allows<br/>login.ts"]:::leaf
    W005["W-005<br/>Authorization is implemented route by route"]:::rootcause
    T005["F-005 - Broken authorization<br/>dataExport.ts"]:::leaf
```

**Structural fixes** (full detail in [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)):

- **[W-001](#w-001)** — Replace evaluation of application values with a data parser or a fixed set of operations whose arguments remain data _(closes [F-010](#f-010))_
- **[W-002](#w-002)** — Provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries _(closes [F-003](#f-003), [F-009](#f-009))_
- **[W-005](#w-005)** — Enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access _(closes [F-005](#f-005))_

---

## 1. System Overview

Probably the most modern and sophisticated insecure web application

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`
**Runtime:** Node\.js 22 - 26

### Scope

This threat model covers 9 components of juice-shop3: 

- CI/CD Pipeline
- Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface
- Real-time WebSocket Channel
- Juice Shop Express Server
- Angular Frontend
- Authentication & Session Surface
- SQLite Store
- Sequelize application data access
- MarsDB Store

**Not individually examined:** 1 source file in 1 component had no route, lead or security signal (CI/CD Pipeline 1).

**Not analysed:** 92 TypeScript files the build leaves out, so their routes and code sites were not checked (`data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_1.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_2.ts` and 82 more).

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** An automated, AI-assisted threat model built from the repository's source code and configuration - static analysis only, no dynamic or penetration testing. It describes the system as built, not as designed.

---

<a id="identified-actors"></a>
### Identified Actors

Figure 1a draws **Internet Attacker** as attacker and **Juice Shop User** and **Juice Shop Admin** as legitimate roles of Juice Shop. [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) draws **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** and the build it attacks. Each row gives that actor's access and the findings attributed to it.

| Actor | Type | Access | Findings |
|----------------------|---------------|----------------------|-----------------|
| Internet Attacker | Attacker | Can self-register a regular account | 🔴 4 · 🟠 46 · 🟡 73 |
| Supply-Chain / Build Attacker | Attacker | Compromises a dependency or build pipeline,<br/>or contributes malicious code to a public<br/>repo | 🟡 8 |
| Juice Shop User | Role (victim) | Person using the client application,<br/>evidenced by its browser entry point. | - |
| Juice Shop Admin | Privileged role | Application role with elevated rights. | - |

3 findings (3 Medium) have no assigned actor. That does not mean they are out of reach.

---

<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-10"></a>tb-10 · Angular Frontend → external</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">An access token obtained from Google is trusted only after the identity it returns is verified by the server rather than the browser.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Egress content</em><br/>🟡 <a href="#f-114">F-114</a> — OAuth access token carried in URL fragment and query string<br/><em>Response trust</em><br/>🟠 <a href="#f-014">F-014</a> — OAuth account password predictably derived</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-11"></a>tb-11 · Juice Shop Express Server → external</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">The server fetches only URLs that point to permitted public hosts.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Egress destination</em><br/>🟠 <a href="#f-045">F-045</a> — Server-side request forgery</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-2"></a>tb-2 · External → Real-time WebSocket Channel</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every Socket.IO event payload is treated as attacker-controlled before it influences server state.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Socket.IO server CORS origin setting</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Authentication</em><br/>🟡 <a href="#f-065">F-065</a> — Server trusts unauthenticated client-reported<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-093">F-093</a> — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-5"></a>tb-5 · External → CI/CD Pipeline</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every third-party action used by a workflow is referenced by a full commit SHA, not a mutable tag.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">SHA-pinned action references</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Authentication</em><br/>🟡 <a href="#f-074">F-074</a> — Third-party GitHub Actions referenced by mutable branch and tags</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-7"></a>tb-7 · External → Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every <code>/rest/web3</code> request is treated as attacker-controlled and checked before the handler acts on it.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Unverified:</strong> 2 related findings</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-9"></a>tb-9 · Sequelize application data access → SQLite Store</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every query reaches SQLite through Sequelize parameter binding.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Sequelize parameter binding</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>No gap shown,</strong> control not confirmed</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-12"></a>tb-12 · Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface → external</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Contract events received from the RPC endpoint are not trusted as proof of a wallet's actions without verification.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Not identified</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>No gap shown,</strong> control not confirmed</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Crossing types, source evidence and provenance are retained in `security-assessment.yaml`.

Settings of the hosting platform at check time, not of the examined source revision. `set` means configured, not effective. `not checkable` means unknown, not missing protection.

Repository selection 1: skipped (local repository; supply a GitHub or GitLab URL to check its platform settings).

## 2. Architecture Diagrams

### 2.1 System Context

Who uses and attacks Juice Shop, and which external systems it exchanges data with. Solid arrows name the data a flow carries; dashed red arrows are attack routes. Actors carry the names Figure 1 uses (C4 Level 1).

```mermaid

    A0["Internet Attacker"]
    A1["Supply-Chain / Build Attacker"]

    R3["Juice Shop Admin"]

    end

    E2["Ethereum Sepolia RPC<br/>(eth-sepolia.g.alchemy.com)"]
    E3["Configured chatbot LLM provider"]

    A1 -.->|"via build pipeline"| SYSTEM

    SYSTEM -->|"Solution notification (HTTP)"| E1
    SYSTEM -->|"NFT mint events · Wallet contract events<br/>(WebSocket)"| E2
    SYSTEM -->|"Chat prompts (HTTP)"| E3
    SYSTEM -->|"OAuth authorization · OAuth profile<br/>request (HTTPS)"| E4
    classDef user     fill:#e8f1ea,stroke:#2e7d32,color:#1b5e20,stroke-width:1.5px
```

**Key takeaway:** Juice Shop serves Juice Shop User and Juice Shop Admin and depends on User-supplied image URL host, Configured solutions webhook, Ethereum Sepolia RPC (eth-sepolia.g.alchemy.com), Configured chatbot LLM provider and Identity provider · accounts.google.com; Internet Attacker and Supply-Chain / Build Attacker attack it.

*🔴 **Unsafe**: relied upon but defeated or trivially bypassable - fix it · 🔴 **Missing**: never built - add it · 🟠 **Weak**: present, with exploitable gaps · 🟡 **Partial**: covers some surfaces, meaningful gaps remain · 🟢 **Adequate**: present and sound. A control counts for the component whose paths hold its implementation evidence. A finding counts for every component it affects, so the columns can add up to more than the register. [§6](#6-security-architecture) holds the full assessment.*

**Key takeaway:** 1 component has at least one control rated Unsafe or Missing (C-09); 5 components have no component-specific control (C-02, C-03, C-04, C-05, C-06); 25 controls apply system-wide.

> **Legend:** `-->` synchronous request/response (REST, HTTPS, gRPC) · `-.->` asynchronous / event-driven (WebSocket, queue, pub-sub) · **red border** ≥ 3 Critical threats on the component · **amber border** ≥ 2 High threats

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the highest-risk findings are exploited - one short walkthrough per Critical, each with attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. Which root cause each Critical traces back to is in [Critical Findings by Root Cause](#critical-findings-by-root-cause); full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals) is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 Broken authorization in Data Export

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `routes/dataExport.ts:26`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request that reaches the code at `routes/dataExport.ts:26`.
2. A signed-in attacker puts another user's ID into the owner field of the request (`req.body.UserId` and the like); the query trusts it instead of the session identity, so the attacker reads or changes every other user's records - horizontal privilege escalation across all accounts.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express Server - routes/dataExport.ts:26
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/dataExport.ts line 26
    App->>App: Broken authorization
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-639
    alt Current state — F-005
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-005
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-144 — Manual review: verify Broken authorization attacker-controll
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-144](#m-144) (Manual review: verify Broken authorization attacker-controlled owner ID in resource query…) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Broken authorization is exploitable at `routes/dataExport.ts:26` (Critical-severity, [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-144](#m-144) (Manual review: verify Broken authorization attacker-controlled owner ID in resource query…)
- Defence in depth: ◕ [M-135](#m-135) (Derive record owner from the session, not the request body)

### 3.2 SQL injection in login credential query allows authentication bypass

**Source:** 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-009](#f-009) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I POST `/rest/user/login` with email set to admin@juice-`sh.op`'-- and any password.
2. The injected quote closes the email literal and the comment drops the password predicate, so the query returns the admin user.
3. The handler signs a JWT for that row and I use it as a bearer token for admin APIs.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Authentication & Session Surface - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: POST /rest/user/login with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-009
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-009
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-167 — Use parameterized queries for login lookup
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-167](#m-167) (Use parameterized queries for login lookup) lands, 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — SQL injection in login credential query allows is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-167](#m-167) (Use parameterized queries for login lookup)

### 3.3 SQL injection in product search query

**Source:** 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — `routes/search.ts:23`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-003](#f-003) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I send GET `/rest/products/search?q=qwert`')) `UNION SELECT id,email,password,4,5,6,7,8,9 FROM Users`-- without logging in.
2. The server concatenates my q into the LIKE clause and runs the query.
3. The JSON response returns every user email and password hash, which I then crack offline.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Juice Shop Express Server - routes/search.ts:23
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: GET /rest/products/search with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-003
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-003
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-166 — Use parameterized replacements for search criteria
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-166](#m-166) (Use parameterized replacements for search criteria) lands, 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection in product search query is exploitable at `routes/search.ts:23` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-166](#m-166) (Use parameterized replacements for search criteria)

### 3.4 Server-side code execution from stored username in User Profile

**Source:** 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — `routes/userProfile.ts:61`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-010](#f-010) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I log in and POST `/profile` with username=#{`global.process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id').toString()`}.
2. `updateUserProfile` stores this username unmodified on my user record.
3. I open GET `/profile`; the handler matches #{...} and calls `eval()` on my code, so the command output is rendered back to me.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express Server - routes/userProfile.ts:61
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/userProfile.ts line 61
    App->>App: Server-side code execution from stored username
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-95
    alt Current state — F-010
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-010
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-168 — Remove eval and pass username as Pug locals
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-168](#m-168) (Remove eval and pass username as Pug locals) lands, 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Server-side code execution from stored username is exploitable at `routes/userProfile.ts:61` (Critical-severity, [CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-168](#m-168) (Remove eval and pass username as Pug locals)

### 3.5 JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist (algorithm confusion)

**Source:** 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — `routes/verify.ts:120`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-013](#f-013) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request that reaches the code at `routes/verify.ts:120`.
2. The attacker sends it; the missing control never rejects the crafted input.
3. Without an explicit algorithm allowlist, attackers can forge tokens with `alg:none` (older lib versions) or use the public key as an HMAC secret to mint valid signatures.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express Server - routes/verify.ts:120
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/verify.ts line 120
    App->>App: JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-347
    alt Current state — F-013
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-013
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-136 — Pin allowed JWT algorithms on verification
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-136](#m-136) (Pin allowed JWT algorithms on verification) lands, 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist is exploitable at `routes/verify.ts:120` (High-severity, [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-136](#m-136) (Pin allowed JWT algorithms on verification)
- Defence in depth: ◑ [M-145](#m-145) (Manual review: verify JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist)

### 3.6 User registration accepts a client-supplied admin role

**Source:** 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — `server.ts:408`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-059](#f-059) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Send POST `/api/Users` with role set to admin in the JSON body.
2. Finale creates the User with role admin because no field filter exists.
3. Log in with the new account and receive a token carrying role admin.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express Server - server.ts:408
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 408
    App->>App: User registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-059
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-059
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-204 — Strip role and privileged fields from registration input
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-204](#m-204) (Strip role and privileged fields from registration input) lands, 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — User registration accepts a client-supplied admin role is exploitable at `server.ts:408` (High-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-204](#m-204) (Strip role and privileged fields from registration input)

### 3.7 Mass assignment of role during user registration in Juice Shop Express Server

**Source:** 🟠 [F-060](#f-060) — `server.ts:484`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-060](#f-060) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An anonymous attacker sends POST `/api/Users` with email, password, `passwordRepeat` and role set to admin.
2. The middlewares that run before finale (`server.ts` lines 408-422) only trim the email and password fields and record challenges. `registerAdminChallenge` and `verify.ts:53` read role==='admin' only to detect or mark the challenge.
3. The finale User resource at `server.ts:484` builds the user from the whole body.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express Server - server.ts:484
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 484
    App->>App: Mass assignment of role during user registration
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-060
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-060
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-205 — Allowlist registration fields and force customer role
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-205](#m-205) (Allowlist registration fields and force customer role) lands, 🟠 [F-060](#f-060) — Mass assignment of role during user registration is exploitable at `server.ts:484` (High-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-205](#m-205) (Allowlist registration fields and force customer role)

### 3.8 Hardcoded JWT signing private key and HMAC keys in source

**Source:** 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-015](#f-015) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. The `RS256` private key that `authorize()` uses to sign every session token is a literal at `lib/insecurity.ts:21`, and it is never rotated.
2. The public key is read from a file (line 20), but the private key has no external store.
3. The survey adds that the security-answer HMAC key (line 42) and the `deluxeToken` HMAC key are also literals.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant Repo
    participant App
    Note over Repo: Public repository
    Note over App: Authentication & Session Surface - lib/insecurity.ts:21
    Attacker->>Repo: Clone or browse - extract private key from lib/insecurity.ts line 21
    Attacker->>Attacker: Sign forged token / signature with the extracted key
    Attacker->>App: Submit forged artefact in normal authentication flow
    App-->>Attacker: Request accepted as authentic - signature validates
    alt Current state — F-015
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker sends the exploit for F-015
        Repo-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-171 — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (F-015)
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        Repo-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-171](#m-171) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (🟠 [F-015](#f-015))) lands, 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — Hardcoded JWT signing private key and HMAC keys in source is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (High-severity, [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-171](#m-171) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (🟠 [F-015](#f-015)))

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_Cataloged controls: 34 total - 2 adequate, 13 partial, 10 weak, 4 unsafe, 5 missing._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What to do |
|----------|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| 🔴 Unsafe | Present and relied upon, but defeated or<br/>trivially bypassable | Fix the existing control |
| 🔴 Missing | Control was never built | Add the control |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing, keep it |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

### 6.2 Identity and Authentication Controls

<a id="ctrl-identity-and-authentication-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-003](#w-003), [W-011](#w-011), [W-012](#w-012), [W-013](#w-013), [W-014](#w-014)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow login](#621-oauth-implicit-flow-login)
- [6.2.2 Second-factor (TOTP) gating at login](#622-second-factor-totp-gating-at-login)

- [6.2.4 Password Reset](#624-password-reset)

**Implemented controls:** OAuth implicit flow login, Hardcoded test credentials in client, Second-factor (TOTP) gating at login, User Registration, Password Reset.

**Assessment:** Registration, reset and login are all defeated: registration accepts a client-chosen admin role (🟠 [F-059](#f-059)), reset accepts a security answer (🟠 [F-021](#f-021)) and leaks the stored hash (🟠 [F-046](#f-046)), and OAuth uses the implicit flow (🟡 [F-069](#f-069)). TOTP gating is partial. Each successful flow above terminates in the server issuing a session token, described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).

<a id="websocket-connection-authentication"></a>
**WebSocket connection authentication.**


**Status:** 🔴 Missing - the `Socket.IO` connection handler accepts every client with no token, session or handshake check.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The socket.io connection handler accepts every connection without a token, session or handshake check. Any client that can reach the server receives stored challenge notifications and can emit handler events. The inspected file has no io.use middleware. Unread middleware elsewhere is not excluded. -->
Any client that can reach the server is treated as trusted:

- Stored challenge notifications, including flags, are emitted to every new connection (🟡 [F-093](#f-093)).
- Unauthenticated sockets can trigger `verify*` handlers that mark challenges solved (🟡 [F-065](#f-065)).
- Unauthenticated events alter shared global state (🟡 [F-091](#f-091)).
- The inspected file has no `io.use` middleware. Middleware elsewhere was not read.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="oauth-implicit-flow-login"></a>
#### 6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow login

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - implicit flow with no state or PKCE, though the redirect URI is checked against an allowlist.

Google login runs in the Angular frontend with `response_type=token`. `oauth.component.ts` reads the access token from the redirect, fetches the profile and signs the user in through the local login endpoint.

The diagram shows how the frontend OAuth adapter ends in the local login flow:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as OAuth Component
    participant Google as Google UserInfo
    participant API as Local User and Login API
    User->>SPA: Return from redirect with access token
    SPA->>Google: Fetch profile
    Google-->>SPA: Email address
    SPA->>API: Register local user
    SPA->>API: Login with derived password
    API-->>SPA: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Google login uses response_type=token (implicit flow), with no state or PKCE parameter. The redirect URI is checked against a configured allowlist (lines 80-87). The access token is passed in a URL query string to the userinfo endpoint. -->
The flow treats OAuth as an identity hint and converts it into a local password login:

- No `state` or PKCE parameter, so the redirect is open to login CSRF (🟡 [F-069](#f-069)).
- The access token travels in a URL query string to the userinfo endpoint (🟡 [F-114](#f-114)).
- The local password is derived predictably from the email (🟠 [F-014](#f-014)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="hardcoded-test-credentials-in-client"></a>
**Hardcoded test credentials in client.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - test account credentials ship in the client bundle.

`login.component.ts` defines a testing username and password as literals in the shipped Angular bundle. The server login route recognizes the same pair.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Test account credentials are embedded in the shipped client bundle. This is a training application, so the exposure may be intentional. -->
The credentials are readable by anyone who loads the frontend. This is a training application, so the exposure may be intentional.

- Client bundle literals (🟡 [F-115](#f-115), 🟡 [F-116](#f-116)).
- Cleartext account passwords in the login handler (🟡 [F-121](#f-121)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="second-factor-totp-gating-at-login"></a>
#### 6.2.2 Second-factor (TOTP) gating at login

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - login returns a temporary token when a TOTP secret is set, but the gate sits behind an injectable credential query.

When a user has a TOTP secret, login returns a temporary token instead of a session token. Second-factor verification happens in the 2FA route, `routes/2fa.ts`.

The diagram shows the positive login path with the branch into TOTP verification:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant TFA as 2FA Route
    User->>API: Email and password
    API-->>User: Temporary token when TOTP secret is set
    User->>TFA: Temporary token and TOTP code
    TFA-->>User: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): When a TOTP secret is set, login returns a temporary token rather than a session token. The check runs only after the SQL-injectable credential query, and the TOTP verification handler was not inspected. -->
The possession factor is only as strong as what sits around it:

- The check runs after the SQL-injectable credential query, so the first factor can be bypassed (🔴 [F-009](#f-009)).
- TOTP secrets are stored in plaintext (🟡 [F-120](#f-120), 🟡 [F-124](#f-124)).
- The reset response leaks the TOTP secret (🟠 [F-047](#f-047)) and the session JWT carries it (🟡 [F-118](#f-118)).
- The verification handler itself was not inspected.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="user-registration"></a>
#### 6.2.3 User Registration

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - `POST /api/Users` accepts a client-chosen admin role.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Present at POST /api/Users -->
`POST /api/Users`, served by a finale resource in `server.ts`, creates accounts from the request body. The User model accepts and sets the `role` attribute.

The diagram shows the intended registration path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Users Endpoint
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: POST email and password
    API->>DB: Create user
    DB-->>API: New user record
    API-->>User: Created user without password
```

**Security assessment**

Registration has no field allowlist on the request body:

- A body `role` of admin reaches the create path (🟠 [F-059](#f-059), 🟠 [F-060](#f-060), 🟠 [F-061](#f-061)).
- No minimum password strength is checked (🟡 [F-068](#f-068)).



**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-055](#f-055) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — User registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
- 🟠 [F-060](#f-060) — Mass assignment of role during user registration
- 🟠 [F-061](#f-061) — Registration lets the request body set the admin role
- 🟡 [F-065](#f-065) — Server trusts unauthenticated client-reported
- 🟡 [F-068](#f-068) — No minimum password strength on registration
- 🟡 [F-091](#f-091) — Unauthenticated websocket events alter global

<a id="password-reset"></a>
#### 6.2.4 Password Reset

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - reset works by security answer, the rate limit is spoofable and the response leaks the password hash.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Present at POST /rest/user/reset-password -->
`routes/resetPassword.ts` handles `POST /rest/user/reset-password`. The user submits an email, a security answer and a new password, and the HMAC of the answer is compared with the stored value.

The diagram shows the intended reset path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Reset Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: Email, security answer, new password
    API->>DB: Compare answer HMAC with stored value
    DB-->>API: Match
    API->>DB: Update password
    API-->>User: Success response
```

**Security assessment**

Recovery rests on weak knowledge factors and leaks data on success:

- A security-question answer is enough to reset (🟠 [F-021](#f-021), 🟠 [F-022](#f-022)).
- The rate limit is keyed on a spoofable forwarded address (🟡 [F-067](#f-067)), and login has no throttling (🟡 [F-127](#f-127)).
- The response returns the password hash and TOTP secret (🟠 [F-046](#f-046), 🟠 [F-047](#f-047)).
- Security questions are disclosed per email (🟡 [F-098](#f-098)).



**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Password reset by security answer with spoofable
- 🟠 [F-046](#f-046) — Password reset response leaks password hash
- 🟠 [F-047](#f-047) — Password reset response leaks hash and TOTP secret
- 🟡 [F-067](#f-067) — Reset-password rate limit keyed on spoofable forwarded address
- 🟡 [F-098](#f-098) — Security question lookup discloses questions
- 🟡 [F-127](#f-127) — No brute-force throttling on login and password reset

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Cookie-token session lookup for profile routes](#631-cookie-token-session-lookup-for-profile-routes)
- [6.3.2 Client-side session token storage](#632-client-side-session-token-storage)
- [6.3.3 Session Token Issuance](#633-session-token-issuance)

**Implemented controls:** Cookie-token session lookup for profile routes, Client-side session token storage, Session Token Issuance (JWT Based).

**Assessment:** This application uses a single locally-signed token format (commonly called JWT) for every authenticated session, regardless of the login flow in [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls). The sub-sections trace cookie lookup, browser storage and issuance. The signing key is embedded in source (🟠 [F-015](#f-015)), verification pins no algorithm (🟠 [F-020](#f-020)) and the token sits in script-readable storage (🟠 [F-001](#f-001)).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — Session JWT stored in script-readable browser storage
- 🟡 [F-071](#f-071) — Token verification helper never checks expiry
- 🟡 [F-122](#f-122) — Session token cookie set without HttpOnly or Secure

<a id="cookie-token-session-lookup-for-profile-routes"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Cookie-token session lookup for profile routes

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - an in-memory server-side lookup, but with no CSRF protection and a hardcoded cookie secret.

Profile routes read the `token` cookie and look it up in an in-memory `authenticatedUsers` map.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The profile routes look up the token cookie in an in-memory authenticatedUsers map. This is a server-side session check. No CSRF protection is visible for these cookie-authenticated POST routes. The cookieParser secret 'kekse' is hardcoded. The lib/insecurity.ts implementation was not inspected. -->
The lookup is a real server-side check, with gaps around it:

- Cookie-authenticated profile updates have no CSRF protection (🟡 [F-080](#f-080)).
- The `cookie-parser` secret is hardcoded (🟡 [F-111](#f-111)).
- The `lib/insecurity.ts` implementation was not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="client-side-session-token-storage"></a>
#### 6.3.2 Client-side session token storage

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the token is readable by page script, and no HttpOnly or Secure flag is set.

`login.component.ts` writes the JWT to `localStorage` and to a cookie via `cookieService.put` with an 8-hour expiry. The request interceptor reads it from `localStorage` for the `Authorization` header.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The JWT is stored in localStorage and in a cookie set via ngy-cookie with an 8-hour expiry. The code shows no HttpOnly, Secure or SameSite options. Script running in the page can read the token. The token is cleared on login failure. -->
Any script running in the page can read the session token:

- Token held in `localStorage` and a cookie with only an expiry (🟠 [F-001](#f-001)).
- Cookie set without HttpOnly or Secure (🟡 [F-122](#f-122)).
- Client-side guards trust unverified claims from that token (🟡 [F-145](#f-145)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="session-token-issuance"></a><a id="session-token-issuance-jwt-based"></a>
#### 6.3.3 Session Token Issuance

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `RS256` signing with a 6-hour expiry, but the private key is public and tokens have no revocation path.

`lib/insecurity.ts` signs tokens with `RS256` and a 6-hour expiry on login, and `express-jwt` verifies them against the public key. Issued tokens are also held in an in-memory map.

The diagram shows token issuance after a successful login and validation on a protected request:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant Sec as Token Helper
    participant Route as Protected Route
    User->>API: Credentials
    API->>Sec: Sign token with RS256 private key
    Sec-->>API: JWT with 6h expiry
    API-->>User: JWT
    User->>Route: Request with bearer token
    Route->>Sec: Verify against public key
    Sec-->>Route: Claims
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Tokens are RS256-signed with a 6h expiry, and express-jwt verifies them against the public key. The private key is embedded in source, so anyone with repository access can forge tokens. Tokens are held in an in-memory map with no revocation path. -->
The signing algorithm is sound, but key handling and verification are not:

- The private key is embedded in source, so anyone with repository access can mint tokens (🟠 [F-015](#f-015), 🟠 [F-016](#f-016)).
- Verification accepts the algorithm from the token (🟠 [F-013](#f-013), 🟠 [F-020](#f-020), 🟡 [F-070](#f-070)).
- The verification helper never checks expiry (🟡 [F-071](#f-071)).
- The payload carries the password hash and TOTP secret (🟡 [F-118](#f-118)).
- No revocation path exists for the in-memory token map.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-005](#w-005), [W-006](#w-006), [W-024](#w-024), [W-025](#w-025), [W-026](#w-026), [W-027](#w-027)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Wallet ownership verification for NFT mint and wallet exploit challenges](#641-wallet-ownership-verification-for-nft-mint-and-wallet-exploit-challenges)
- [6.4.2 Route-level authorization middleware](#642-route-level-authorization-middleware)
- [6.4.3 LLM chatbot tool authorization and coupon policy](#643-llm-chatbot-tool-authorization-and-coupon-policy)
- [6.4.4 Role checks](#644-role-checks)

**Implemented controls:** Wallet ownership verification for NFT mint and wallet exploit challenges, Route-level authorization middleware (`isAuthorized`/`denyAll`/`isAccounting`), LLM chatbot tool authorization and coupon policy, Role checks (accounting/deluxe/customer).

**Assessment:** Guards are applied route by route, so coverage is uneven. Object ownership is missing on basket, user and review resources (🟠 [F-002](#f-002), 🟡 [F-137](#f-137), 🟡 [F-138](#f-138), 🟡 [F-135](#f-135)), and the product update guard is commented out (🟠 [F-057](#f-057)). Registration can set the role (🟠 [F-059](#f-059)), and client-side guards trust unverified claims (🟡 [F-145](#f-145)).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-002](#f-002) — Missing ownership checks on basket endpoints allow cross-user basket access
- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Broken authorization
- 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — IDOR
- 🟠 [F-055](#f-055) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟠 [F-057](#f-057) — Unauthenticated product update
- 🟠 [F-058](#f-058) — LLM coupon tool lacks server-side discount
- 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — User registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
- 🟠 [F-060](#f-060) — Mass assignment of role during user registration
- 🟠 [F-061](#f-061) — Registration lets the request body set the admin role
- 🟠 [F-062](#f-062) — Address update by id lacks owner check
- 🟠 [F-063](#f-063) — Authentication details list returns all users without role check
- 🟡 [F-065](#f-065) — Server trusts unauthenticated client-reported
- 🟡 [F-082](#f-082) — Expired campaign coupon accepted
- 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — Unauthenticated product modification
- 🟡 [F-084](#f-084) — Unauthenticated review creation takes
- 🟡 [F-085](#f-085) — Feedback creation trusts a body-supplied UserId
- 🟡 [F-109](#f-109) — Orders looked up by non-unique vowel-masked e-mail
- 🟡 [F-110](#f-110) — User directory exposed to any authenticated user
- 🟡 [F-131](#f-131) — Any authenticated user can delete any customer feedback
- 🟡 [F-132](#f-132) — Any authenticated user can create catalogue products
- 🟡 [F-133](#f-133) — Duplicate JSON keys bypass BasketId ownership check
- 🟡 [F-134](#f-134) — Basket item creation lets a duplicate BasketId target
- 🟡 [F-135](#f-135) — Review update lacks author ownership check
- 🟡 [F-136](#f-136) — Any authenticated user can edit other users'
- 🟡 [F-137](#f-137) — Basket items readable and deletable across users by id
- 🟡 [F-138](#f-138) — Any authenticated user can read any user record by id
- 🟡 [F-140](#f-140) — Server access logs are downloadable without any authorization
- 🟡 [F-141](#f-141) — Deluxe membership granted without payment verification
- 🟡 [F-142](#f-142) — Client-supplied UserId accepted on feedback and complaint
- 🟡 [F-143](#f-143) — Security answer creation accepts owner UserId from request body
- 🟡 [F-145](#f-145) — Client-side role guards trust unverified JWT claims
- 🟡 [F-146](#f-146) — Missing role check on authenticated user
- 🟡 [F-200](#f-200) — Shopper tampers with another customer's basket or order

<a id="wallet-ownership-verification-for-nft-mint-and-wallet-exploit-challenges"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Wallet ownership verification for NFT mint and wallet exploit challenges

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - ownership rests on a request-body wallet address with no signature check.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): routes/nftMint.ts walletNFTVerify and routes/web3Wallet.ts contractExploitListener -->
`routes/nftMint.ts` and `routes/web3Wallet.ts` take `walletAddress` from the request body and check it against in-memory sets of on-chain minters or exploiters.

The diagram shows the intended wallet claim path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Wallet Route
    participant Set as In-memory Minter Set
    User->>API: POST walletAddress
    API->>Set: Look up address
    Set-->>API: Known minter
    API-->>User: Challenge solved
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The wallet address comes from the request body (req.body.walletAddress). The code does not verify signature-based ownership and does not authenticate the caller. The address is checked only against an in-memory set of on-chain event minters or exploiters, so anyone who knows a minter's address can claim that mint. The web3Wallet route adds unvalidated, unbounded input to an in-memory Set. The code shows no authentication or rate limiting on these handlers. Middleware in server.ts was not inspected, so it may enforce some. -->
The wallet address is a claim, not a proof:

- No signature verifies ownership and the caller is not authenticated, so anyone who knows a minter address can claim the mint.
- Input is added to an unbounded in-memory set (🟡 [F-125](#f-125)).
- Middleware in `server.ts` was not inspected and may enforce some checks.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="route-level-authorization-middleware"></a><a id="route-level-authorization-middleware-isauthorizeddenyallisaccounting"></a>
#### 6.4.2 Route-level authorization middleware

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - per-route guards cover baskets, B2B and accounting, but coverage is uneven.

`server.ts` applies `isAuthorized`, `denyAll` and `isAccounting` per route. Baskets, BasketItems, B2B and accounting routes are guarded, and many admin-style resources use `denyAll`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Authorization is applied per route. Baskets, BasketItems, B2B and accounting routes are guarded, and many admin-style resources use denyAll. Coverage is uneven: GET /api/Users, /rest/order-history, /rest/wallet/balance, /rest/memories and /rest/chat have no isAuthorized in the inspected lines. The POST /api/Products guard is present while the PUT guard is commented out. The finale-generated endpoints are only partly covered. I did not inspect the implementation of isAuthorized. -->
Guards are attached route by route, so gaps appear wherever one is forgotten:

- The product PUT guard is commented out (🟠 [F-057](#f-057), 🟡 [F-083](#f-083)).
- The user directory and authentication-details list are open to any authenticated user (🟡 [F-110](#f-110), 🟠 [F-063](#f-063)).
- The memories endpoint returns full user records unauthenticated (🟠 [F-040](#f-040)).
- Sensitive routes are registered without middleware (🟠 [F-055](#f-055)).
- The `isAuthorized` implementation was not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="llm-chatbot-tool-authorization-and-coupon-policy"></a>
#### 6.4.3 LLM chatbot tool authorization and coupon policy

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - coupon limits exist only in the system prompt, not in server code.

`routes/chat.ts` exposes tools to the model, including `getOrderById`, which checks ownership through the authenticated user, and `generateCoupon`. The 10% coupon cap and verified-order rule are stated in the system prompt.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The coupon policy (10% maximum, verified order) is enforced only in the system prompt. The generateCoupon tool executes any model-chosen discount with no server-side cap or order check. getOrderById does check ownership via the authenticated user. getProductReviews builds a \$where string from Number(id), which is only partly safe. The route has no authentication requirement or rate limit. Inspected scope: routes/chat.ts lines 78-198. -->
The tool layer trusts the model to apply policy:

- `generateCoupon` runs any model-chosen discount with no server-side cap (🟠 [F-058](#f-058), 🟡 [F-144](#f-144)).
- Client-supplied chat history reaches the model (🟠 [F-031](#f-031)).
- Order lookup trusts identity from an unverified JWT (🟠 [F-012](#f-012)).
- The route has no authentication requirement or rate limit (🟠 [F-049](#f-049)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="role-checks"></a><a id="role-checks-accountingdeluxecustomer"></a>
#### 6.4.4 Role checks

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - roles are read from the JWT after signature verification, but key and algorithm weaknesses undermine them.

Role checks verify the JWT signature and then read the role claim. Deluxe status also requires an HMAC token keyed with the hardcoded private key.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Role checks verify the JWT signature and then read the role claim. Deluxe status additionally requires an HMAC token keyed with the hardcoded private key. appendUserId trusts the in-memory tokenMap. -->
Role decisions inherit the token weaknesses:

- Verification accepts the token's algorithm (🟠 [F-013](#f-013)), and the signing key is public (🟠 [F-015](#f-015)).
- Angular guards decode claims without verification (🟡 [F-145](#f-145)).
- The authenticated-users route has no role check (🟡 [F-146](#f-146)).
- `appendUserId` trusts the in-memory token map.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 Credentials sent in GET query string](#651-credentials-sent-in-get-query-string)
- [6.5.2 Login query construction](#652-login-query-construction)
- [6.5.3 Sequelize ORM data access layer and embedded SQLite configuration](#653-sequelize-orm-data-access-layer-and-embedded-sqlite-configuration)
- [6.5.4 Embedded MarsDB data-store access control](#654-embedded-marsdb-data-store-access-control)

**Implemented controls:** Credentials sent in GET query string, Login query construction, Sequelize ORM data access layer and embedded SQLite configuration.

**Assessment:** Login and product search build SQL by string concatenation (🔴 [F-009](#f-009), 🔴 [F-003](#f-003)). Review and order lookups accept NoSQL operators and `$where` strings (🟠 [F-029](#f-029) (NoSQL \$where JavaScript injection in product reviews and order tracking), 🟡 [F-086](#f-086) — NoSQL operator injection in review update and like selectors), and the MarsDB collections have no access control of their own.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection in product search query
- 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — SQL injection in login credential query allows
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — NoSQL \$where JavaScript injection in product reviews and order tracking
- 🟡 [F-086](#f-086) — NoSQL operator injection in review update and like selectors

<a id="credentials-sent-in-get-query-string"></a>
#### 6.5.1 Credentials sent in GET query string

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - passwords are sent in a URL, where logs and history can retain them.

`user.service.ts` sends the current and new passwords as URL query parameters in a GET request to `/rest/user/change-password`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): changePassword sends the current and new passwords as URL query parameters in a GET request. URLs can end up in logs and browser history, and a GET request is not protected against CSRF. -->
Putting secrets in the URL exposes them and the GET form is open to CSRF:

- Current and new passwords travel in the query string (🟡 [F-113](#f-113), 🟡 [F-107](#f-107)).
- The change skips a current-password check (🟠 [F-051](#f-051)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="login-query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.2 Login query construction

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the email is concatenated into raw SQL, so the credential check can be bypassed.

`routes/login.ts` builds the credential query as a raw SQL string. The password is `MD5`-hashed first, while the email is concatenated as submitted.

The diagram shows the intended password-login path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: Email and password
    API->>DB: Query by email and password hash
    DB-->>API: Matching user row
    API-->>User: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The login query concatenates req.body.email directly into raw SQL, so the credential check can be bypassed by SQL injection. The password is MD5-hashed before concatenation, but the email field is unescaped. -->
Two independent weaknesses sit on the login path:

- Email is concatenated into SQL, so injection returns an arbitrary user row (🔴 [F-009](#f-009)).
- Passwords are unsalted `MD5` (🟠 [F-048](#f-048) — Passwords stored as unsalted MD5 hashes (Passwords stored as unsalted `MD5` hashes)), and login has no brute-force throttling (🟡 [F-127](#f-127)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="sequelize-orm-data-access-layer-and-embedded-sqlite-configuration"></a>
#### 6.5.3 Sequelize ORM data access layer and embedded SQLite configuration

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - Sequelize is centralized, but SQLite offers no database-level authentication.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): models/index.ts: createSequelize() and initModels() -->
`models/index.ts` initializes models through `createSequelize()` and `initModels()` against the embedded SQLite file `data/juiceshop.sqlite`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Models are initialized centrally through Sequelize against an embedded SQLite file (data/juiceshop.sqlite). The connection uses placeholder credentials ('username'/'password') that SQLite ignores, so there is no database-level authentication or access control. Data protection depends entirely on application-process and filesystem controls. The database file has no encryption at rest in this configuration. Only models/index.ts was inspected; per-model setters and query construction in the other model files were not reviewed, so no rating is given for them. -->
Data protection depends on the application process and filesystem:

- Placeholder credentials are ignored by SQLite, so there is no database access control.
- Stored card numbers are unencrypted (🟡 [F-123](#f-123)).
- Raw SQL in search bypasses the model layer (🔴 [F-003](#f-003)).
- Other model files were not reviewed.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="embedded-marsdb-data-store-access-control"></a>
#### 6.5.4 Embedded MarsDB data-store access control

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - collections are plain in-process objects with no access control or schema validation.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The reviews and orders collections are instantiated directly as in-process MarsDB collections with no authentication, schema validation, or per-collection access control at this layer. Any code in the application process can read or write them, so access control and query sanitisation depend entirely on callers. Callers were not inspected here. -->
Reviews and orders are in-process MarsDB collections, so protection depends entirely on callers:

- Update and like selectors accept NoSQL operators (🟡 [F-086](#f-086)).
- `$where` JavaScript injection reaches reviews and order tracking (🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — NoSQL \$where JavaScript injection in product reviews and order tracking (NoSQL \$where JavaScript injection in product reviews and order tracking)).
- Callers were not inspected here.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.6.1 Validation Approach](#661-validation-approach)
